. Following Generation
4.5
, the key generation step undertaken by
\mathsf{Alice}
for this cryptosystem consists of:

-

a non-empty discrete category
I=[n]
containing
n
objects (and their identities) and a functor

H:\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}I&\to&T_{\mathbb{Z}[X]}^{\mathsf{op}}\\
k&\mapsto&1\end{array}\right)

picking out the object
1
in the limit sketch
T_{\mathbb{Z}[X]}
;

-

an initializer
f_{0}
taken in the following set:

\mathsf{lim}_{I^{\mathsf{op}}}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}\circ H^{\mathsf{op}})=\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N\times n)}

-

and a private key
x
taken in the following
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module:

\mathsf{col}_{I}(L\circ Y\circ H)(1)=\Big(\coprod^{n}Y(1)\Big)(1)\cong Y(n)(1)\cong\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}

Later, we will use the notations
f_{0}=(f_{0,i,j})_{i,j}
and
x=(x_{j})_{j}
to refer to the components of the elements
f_{0}
and
x
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
and
\mathbb{Z}[X]
, respectively. For convenience, we will also denote
f_{0,i}:=(f_{0,i,j})_{i,j}
for every fixed index
i\in[N]
.

Remark 5.16

(Correctness and security)
.

Let us discuss the form of the elements in the set
\mathcal{R}(f)
, as defined in Generation
5.15
. Stepping back from the specific perspective of Generation
5.15
, we essentially used the definition of the set
M
to show that the inclusion
\mathcal{R}(f)\supseteq f+\mathcal{I}_{k}(\mathsf{C})^{(N)}
holds for every
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}
, where we have the following formula:

k=\left\lfloor\frac{\lfloor(q-1)/p\rfloor}{pN}\right\rfloor.

This inclusion implies that if we take an element
f^{\prime}=f+e
with
e\in\mathcal{I}_{k}(\mathsf{C})^{(N)}
, then the decryption equation
\mathsf{D}(h(f),\mathsf{E}_{R}(h(f^{\prime}),m))=m
holds for every
h\in M
. While this ensures correctness for ACES, a basic level of security is guaranteed only if
k\neq 0
. In fact, the larger
k
is, the more secure ACES becomes.

This is where the inequality
q\geq p^{2}N+1
serves its purpose, as it ensures that
k\geq 1
. However, a more general assumption can be made by requiring

q\geq k_{0}p^{2}N+1

to hold for some positive integer
k_{0}
, thereby ensuring that
k\geq k_{0}
. This generalized inequality can be used to further enhance security.

The following publication step embeds the public key in the free-ring
\mathbb{Z}[X]
. However, in practice, this key would be more efficiently encoded by its representative modulo the integer
q
and the polynomial
u
.

Publication 5.17

(ACES)
.

The key publication step for ACES goes as follows.
\mathsf{Alice}
does the following:

-

she computes the element

f=\phi_{H,\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}}^{-1}(f_{0})_{1}(x)

This element corresponds to the image of the element
x\in\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
via the morphism

\phi_{H,\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}}^{-1}(f_{0}):\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}

that sends every element
y
in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
to the matrix product of
f_{0}
with
y
. As a result, the element
f
is equal to the matrix product
f_{0}x
.

-

Since
\mathcal{R}(f)\supseteq f+\mathcal{I}_{1}(\mathsf{C})^{(N)}
,
\mathsf{Alice}
can send a pair of the form
(f_{0},f+e)
, where
e\in\mathcal{I}_{1}(\mathsf{C})^{(N)}
, to
\mathsf{Bob}
;

For convenience, we will denote the pair sent by
\mathsf{Alice}
to
\mathsf{Bob}
as a tuple
(f_{0},f^{\prime})
.

We now outline the encryption process for ACES. A distinctive feature of the encryption step described below, compared to the encryption schemes detailed thus far, is the careful specification of the set
M
of morphisms, which is crucial for managing the noise introduced into the public keys.

Encryption 5.18

(ACES)
.

The encryption step for ACES is defined as follows. First,
\mathsf{Bob}
selects:

-

a noise parameter
r\in\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{C})
;

-

a morphism
h\in M
of the following type.

h:\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}

Such a morphism is described as a linear map
y\mapsto b^{T}y
where
b
is a fixed element in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}
and, if we denote
b=(b_{j})_{j}
, every component
b_{i}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
satisfies the relation
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(b_{i})\in\{0,1,\dots,p\}
;

-

and a message
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
;

Then,
\mathsf{Bob}
sends the following information to
\mathsf{Alice}
:

-

the element
c_{1}=\mathsf{lim}_{I^{\mathsf{op}}}(h_{H})(f_{0})=f_{0}^{T}b
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]^{(n)}
where
f_{0}^{T}b
denotes the matrix product of the transpose of
f_{0}
with
b
;

-

and the element
c_{2}=\mathsf{E}_{r}(h_{1}(f^{\prime}),m)=r(m)+b^{T}(f_{0}x+e)
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{N}
.

Finally, we conclude the decryption step, whose correctness is ensured by the Yoneda Encryption Scheme.

Decryption 5.19

(ACES)
.

The decryption step for ACES unfolds as follows.
\mathsf{Alice}
computes:

-

the element

d=\phi_{H,\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}}^{-1}(c_{1})_{1}(x).

This element corresponds to the image of the element
x\in\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
via the morphism

\phi_{H,\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}}^{-1}(f_{0}):\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}

that sends every element
y
in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
to the scalar product of
c_{1}
with
y
. As a result, the element
d
is equal to the matrix product
c_{1}^{T}x
.

-

and the element
m^{\prime}=\mathsf{D}(d,c_{2})=\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c_{2}-c_{1}^{T}x)))
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

The equality
m^{\prime}=m
is guaranteed by the calculations of Decryption
4.10
.

5.2.
From polynomials to integers

As suggested by the form of the encryption and decryption functions used in Generation
5.15
, ACES relies on a dual lattice-based formalism, involving both integers and polynomials. While this interaction can also be formally captured by Proposition
5.3
, this section aims to clarify this duality by discussing canonical examples on which the reader can base their intuition.

Remark 5.20

(Double modulo constructions)
.

The rationale behind employing a double modulo operation in most LWE-based cryptosystems can be explained as follows. Let
p
and
q
be two coprime integers such that
p<q
, and let
m
be a message in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. First, note that the equation
(m\,(\mathsf{mod}\,q))\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)=m
holds. This equation can be generalized for every
g\in[q-1-m]
as follows:

\big(m+g\,(\mathsf{mod}\,q)\big)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)=(m+g)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

However, we cannot extend the previous formula to integers
g\in[q-m,+\infty]
. Specifically, for every positive integer
k
and every integer
g\in[q-m,qk-m-1]
, we have the following equation for some positive integer
k_{0}\leq k-1
.

\displaystyle\big(m+g\,(\mathsf{mod}\,q)\big)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

\displaystyle=(m+g-qk_{0})\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

\displaystyle=\big(m^{\prime}+g\big)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

\displaystyle m^{\prime}=(m-qk_{0})\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

Here, we can see that passing
m+g
through the two successive modulo operations conceals the message
m
with a noisy component
qk_{0}\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)
. The presence of this term complicates guessing the actual value of
m
as the range for
g
becomes wider. This is precisely the strategy used by LWE-based cryptosystems for hiding messages.

In Encryption
5.18
, we utilize two components, denoted as
r
and
e
, to obscure the message during encryption. This construction aligns with the strategies employed in cryptographic systems such as BGV, FV, and CKKS. However, a notable distinction between these cryptosystems and ACES is that, in ACES, both noisy components
r
and
e
only affect the part of the ciphertext containing the message. In contrast, in BGV, FV, and CKKS, the corresponding noisy components are distributed across the two parts of the ciphertext.

Remark 5.21

(Evaluation as an attack)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel such that
p
and
q
are coprime. Since the messages of ACES belong to the set
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, it seems tempting to attack the ACES encryption by evaluating ciphertexts through the morphism
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}
, followed by modding the resulting image by
p
. However, by doing so, we would also emphasize the phenomenon described in Remark
5.20
. Indeed, for every polynomial
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
, we can expect that the evaluation
g(\omega)
in
\mathbb{N}
is on average greater than
q
(provided that the degree
d
of
u
is greater than
1
).

This means that, for every message
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and element
r\in\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{C})
, if we sum the polynomial
r(m)
with a polynomial
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
, then the noise component discussed in Remark
5.13
will most likely occur when evaluating the expression
r(m)+g
through the function
x\mapsto\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x)))
.

\displaystyle\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}\big(r(m)+g\big)))

\displaystyle=\big(r(m)(\omega)+g(\omega)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,q)\big)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

\displaystyle=(m+g(\omega)-qk_{0})\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p))

\displaystyle=\big((m-qk_{0})\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)+g(\omega)\big)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

\displaystyle=\big(r(m^{\prime})(\omega)+g(\omega)\big)\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

\displaystyle m^{\prime}=(m-qk_{0})\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)

Here, the noisy component is difficult to guess because the encoding of the polynomial
r
is only known to the sender of the message
m
. Indeed, note that the component
r
is essential in replicating the phenomenon of Remark
5.20
. If the polynomial
r(m)
was trivial, namely equal to
m
, then the coefficients of the polynomial
r(m)+g
would be the same as those of
g
, except for the
0
-th coefficient, which is very likely to be less than
q
as
p
is small. This means that for a trivial
r(m)
, an attacker could potentially determine the value of the noisy component
qk_{0}\,(\mathsf{mod}\,p)
by evaluating
r(m^{\prime})+g
at
\omega
. As a result, the attack could be reduced to finding a representative of
g
modulo
p
.

To conclude, one of the main uses of polynomials in ACES is their ability to generate a broader range of noise, while also providing us with the opportunity to introduce more randomness by expressing integers as decompositions into polynomials. As discussed in Example
5.10
and Example
5.13
, this relationship allows us to shape the integer values in a specific manner while making it difficult to retrieve them when presented in a polynomial expression. We will employ a similar procedure in section
5.3
to further conceal the secret.

5.3.
Homomorphic properties

This section addresses the homomorphic properties of the cryptosystem defined in section
5.1
. Specifically, it exhibits homomorphic properties for addition and multiplication with full traceability of the noise level.

To permit these homomorphic properties, an additional piece of information needs to be disclosed during the publication step of the cryptosystem. Importantly, this extra information is not anticipated to compromise the security of the cryptosystem.

Definition 5.22

(Repartition)
.

Let
q
and
n
be positive integers such that
q
has
n_{0}
distinct prime factors. We will refer to any function of the form
[n]\to\{0,1,2,\dots,n_{0}\}
as an
n
-repartition of
q
. Let now
q_{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{n_{0}}
denote the sequence of prime factors of
q
, ordered in increasing magnitude. We shall also denote
q_{0}=1
. For every
n
-repartition
\sigma:[n]\to\{0\}\cup[n_{0}]
of
q
and every pair
(i,j)
in
[n]
, we use the following notation:

\sigma[q]_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}q/q_{\sigma(i)}q_{\sigma(j)}&\sigma(i)\neq\sigma(j)\\
q/q_{\sigma(i)}&\sigma(i)=\sigma(j)\\
\end{array}\right.

Definition 5.23
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. We denote as
\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
the set of 3-tensors
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})_{i,j,k\in[n]}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
such that, for every pair
(i,j)
of elements in
[n]
, if we denote

(5.4)

e_{i,j}=x_{i}\cdot x_{j}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{i,j}^{k}\cdot x_{k}

then the element
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e_{i,j})
is a multiple of
\sigma[q]_{i,j}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

As suggested by Generation
5.24
, the set defined in Definition
5.23
originates from the observation that
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-modules possess three degrees of freedom: the dimension of the ring of integer, which acts of the formal monomial
X
, the dimension given by polynomials in
\mathbb{Z}[X]
, and the dimension offered by the module structure
1
1

1

This leveled structure is implicitely suggested by our construction in Convention
3.37
, where we (1) start with a ring structure
R
, (2) then send it to a commutative group structure, and (3) induce its associated modules using functors
T_{R}\to\mathbf{Set}
. This distinguishes it from the cryptosystems explored in section
4
, which were typically dependent on what could be considered as two degrees of freedom, often encoded by generators and their exponents (e.g., ElGamal, RSA, Benaloh) or encoded by rings and their ideals (NTRU and LWE-based cryptosystems). In essence, the reader may perceive this freedom in dimensionality as the factor that renders ACES fully homomorphic.

Generation 5.24

(Homomorphism)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. To define an element in
\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
, we can consider the following function of type
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for every pair
(i,j)
of elements in
[n]
:

E_{i,j}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n})=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{j})-\sum_{k=1}^{n}Y_{k}\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{k})

Then, because
\sigma[q]_{i,j}
is a factor of
q
, every element
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})_{i,j,k\in[n]}
in
\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
is characterized by the following relation:

E_{i,j}(\lambda_{i,j}^{1},\dots,\lambda_{i,j}^{n})\equiv 0\,(\mathsf{mod}\,\sigma[q]_{i,j})

Note that the following tuples already provide solutions
(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})_{i,j,k\in[n]}
for the equation
E_{i,j}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})=0
.

(0,\dots,0,\mathop{\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})}\limits_{j\textrm{-th}},0,\dots,0)\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt(0,\dots,0,\mathop{\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{j})}\limits_{i\textrm{-th}},0,\dots,0)

However, we want to steer away from such solutions as they reveal information that can be used to decrypt ciphertexts generated by ACES. More generally, we can sample
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n-1}
randomly as a tuple
(\lambda_{i,j}^{1},\dots,\lambda_{i,j}^{n-1})
and solve the following equation for some random integer
\ell_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
:

Y_{n}\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{n})=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{j})-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\lambda_{i,j}^{k}\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{k})-\ell_{i,j}\cdot\sigma[q]_{i,j}

The previous equation is always solvable when
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{n})
is invertible in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Finally, note that for a given element
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})_{i,j,k\in[n]}
in
\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
, an attacker who discovers the
n
-repartition
\sigma
can attempt to find the images
(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{k}))_{k\in[n]}
by solving the following system of polynomial equations (defined for every pair
(i,j)
in
[n]
).

X_{i}\cdot X_{j}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{i,j}^{k}\cdot X_{k}-L_{i,j}\cdot\sigma[q]_{i,j}=0

This system could potentially be solved by using Gröbner basis reduction techniques. However, by imposing that
\lambda_{i,j}^{k}=\lambda_{j,i}^{k}
and
L_{i,j}=L_{j,i}
, we prevent this scenario from occurring as the equation for
(i,j)
cannot be reduced by the equation for
(j,i)
, as they are exactly the same.

The set of 3-tensors defined in Generation
5.24
are used to define the operation presented in Definition
5.25
. The shape of the 3-tensors used for this operation will specifically allow us to obtain the identity stated in Proposition
5.28
.

Definition 5.25
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every element
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})
in
\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
and every pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
of elements in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(n)}
, we denote as
v_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}v_{2}
the element of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(n)}
encoded by the following tuple.

v_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}v_{2}:=\Big(\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i,j}^{k}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}\Big)_{k\in[n]}

To ensure that the 3-tensors defined in Generation
5.24
can be iteratively used through homomorphic operations (see Definition
5.33
), we will have to restrict them to a subset defined in Definition
5.26

Definition 5.26

(Zero-divisor ideal)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. We will denote as
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-submodule of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(n)}
consisting of tuples
v=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})
such that for every
i\in[n]
, there exists an integer
r_{i}
for which
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(v_{i})
is of the form
q_{\sigma(i)}r_{i}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Similarly, we denote as
\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
the set of
3
-tensors
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
such that for every triple
(i,j,k)
in
[n]
, there exists an integer
r_{i,j}^{k}
such that
\lambda_{i,j}^{k}=q_{\sigma(k)}r_{i,j}^{k}
.

The following remark refines the discussion of Generation
5.24
using arithmetic concepts. In particular, it ensures that the operation defined in Definition
5.25
is stable for the homomorphic framework defined later in Definition
5.33
.

Remark 5.27

(Zero-divisor ideal)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
.
It directly follows from Definition
5.26
and Definition
5.25
that if a 3-tensor
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})
belongs to
\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
, then every
n
-vector of the form
v_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}v_{2}
belongs to
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
.

Note that generating an element
\lambda
in
\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
should be as difficult as using the methods presented in Generation
5.24
to generate an element in
\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
. Specifically, this holds if we assume that the greatest common divisor of the following sequence of
n
integers is equal to
1
.

q_{\sigma(1)}\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{1})),\,q_{\sigma(2)}\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{2})),\,\dots,\,q_{\sigma(n)}\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{n}))

In this case, we can find elements
\mu_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the following Bézout identity holds.

q_{\sigma(1)}\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{1}))\cdot\mu_{1}+q_{\sigma(2)}\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{2}))\cdot\mu_{2}+\dots+q_{\sigma(n)}\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{n}))\cdot\mu_{n}=1

Taking
\ell_{i,j}
as in Generation
5.24
and multiplying the previous equation by the integer
\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i}x_{j}))-\ell_{i,j}\sigma[q]_{i,j}
would then generate an element
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})
in
\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
such that the following equation holds:

\lambda_{i,j}^{k}=q_{\sigma(k)}\mu_{k}\cdot\Big(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{j})-\ell_{i,j}\sigma[q]_{i,j}\Big)

Note that we can mitigate the formal construction of each element
\lambda_{i,j}^{k}
by randomly sampling
\ell_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. However, we must note that the formula

\lambda_{i,i}^{i}\equiv q_{\sigma(i)}\cdot\mu_{i}\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})^{2}\,(\mathsf{mod}\,q)
always holds.

Proposition 5.28
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every element
\lambda=(\lambda_{i,j}^{k})
in
\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
and every pair
(v_{1},v_{2})
of elements in
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
, there exists an element
e
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
such that
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e)=0
and the following equation holds in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
.

(v_{1}^{T}x)\cdot(v_{2}^{T}x)=(v_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}v_{2})^{T}x+e

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward calculation using the different concepts introduced in Definition
5.23
and Definition
5.25
. Specifically, we have:

\displaystyle(v_{1}^{T}x)\cdot(v_{2}^{T}x)

\displaystyle=(\sum_{i}v_{1,i}x_{i})\cdot(\sum_{j}v_{2,j}x_{j})=\sum_{i,j}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}x_{i}x_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}\big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{i,j}^{k}\cdot x_{k}+e_{i,j}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Big(\sum_{i,j}\lambda_{i,j}^{k}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}\Big)\cdot x_{k}+\sum_{i,j}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}e_{i,j}

\displaystyle=(v_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}v_{2})^{T}x+\sum_{i,j}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}e_{i,j}

If we take
e=\sum_{i,j}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}e_{i,j}
, then there exist two tuples
(k_{1,i})_{i\in[n]}
,
(k_{2,i})_{i\in[n]}
of integers in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and a matrix
(k_{3,i,j})_{i,j\in[n]}
of integers in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
such that the following equations hold.

\displaystyle\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e)

\displaystyle=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}\big(\sum_{i,j}v_{1,i}v_{2,j}e_{i,j}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(v_{1,i})\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(v_{2,j})\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e_{i,j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}q_{\sigma(i)}k_{1,i}q_{\sigma(j)}k_{2,j}\sigma[q]_{i,j}k_{3,i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}qk_{1,i}k_{2,j}k_{3,i,j}=0

This proves the proposition.
∎

Note that the message
m
in Definition
5.29
(below) belongs to the set
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, unlike the set
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
used with ACES (see Application
4.62
or Remark
5.30
). As discussed prior to Proposition
5.31
, this distinction influences how elements of encryption spaces are refreshed and, more broadly, decrypted.

Definition 5.29

(Encryption space)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
.
For every element
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and every non-negative integer
k
, we define the
k
-th
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space

\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k}(m|\sigma)
at the element
m
, relative to the repartition
\sigma
, as the set containing the pairs

(c,c^{\prime})\in\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}

for which there exist
r\in\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{C})
and
e\in\mathcal{I}_{k}(C)
such that the equation
c^{\prime}=r(m)+c^{T}x+e
holds in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
.

Note that the owner of the secret key
x
can always use the formula given in Definition
5.29
to construct ciphertexts for given messages. On the other hand, any third party without knowledge of the secret key is constrained to using the ACES formalism described below.

Remark 5.30

(ACES)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel and let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
. Consider an ACES encryption scheme, as defined in section
5.1
(see Generation
5.15
) with a private key
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(n)}
and such that each row of the initializer matrix

f_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}f_{0,1,1}&\dots&f_{0,1,n}\\
\vdots&\dots&\vdots\\
f_{0,N,1}&\dots&f_{0,N,n}\\
\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N\times n)}

is an element of
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
. For every message
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and every encryption
(c_{1},c_{2})
of
m
via this encryption scheme, the pair
(c_{1},c_{2})
belongs to the
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space
\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},Np}(m|\sigma)
. Indeed, as explained in Encryption
5.18
, we have the equations

c_{1}=f_{0}^{T}b\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988ptc_{2}=r(m)+b^{T}(f_{0}x+e)=r(m)+c_{1}^{T}x+b^{T}e

where
e\in\mathcal{I}_{1}(\mathsf{C})^{(N)}
and the vector
b=(b_{1},\dots,b_{N})
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}^{(N)}
is such that
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(b_{i})\in\{0,1,\dots,p\}
for every
i\in[N]
. First, the expression
c_{1}=f_{0}^{T}b
tells us that
c_{1}
is a linear combination of elements in the
\mathbb{Z}[X]
-module
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
and is therefore in
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
. Then, since we have the equation

\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(b^{T}e)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(b_{i}\cdot e_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(b_{i})\cdot\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e_{i})

it follows from Proposition
5.8
that the element
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(b^{T}e)
is in
\chi(Np)
, and hence
b^{T}e
is in
\mathcal{I}_{Np}(\mathsf{C})
. This proves our earlier statement.

Proposition
5.31
, presented below, consists of two independent statements that establish the correctness of ACES. The second statement directly asserts the correctness of the leveled encryption scheme structure associated with ACES. The first statement, on the other hand, enables the exploration of a novel method for ciphertext refreshing, which differs from traditional bootstrapping techniques. Notably, in the first statement, the message
m
is considered in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, whereas in the second, it is taken in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Proposition 5.31

(Decryption)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. Consider the following decryption function, which was introduced in Generation
5.15
.

\mathsf{D}:\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}&\to&\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
(g,c)&\mapsto&\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c-g)))\end{array}\right)

For every element
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, let
\xi_{p}(m)
denote the integer quotient
\lfloor\iota_{q}(m)/p\rfloor
, representing the division of
m
by
p
. Using this notation, the following implication holds:

(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k}(m)\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\Rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k+\xi_{p}(m)}\big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(m)\big).

Also, for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and every non-negative integer
k<(q+1)/p-1
, the following implication is satisfied:

(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k}(m)\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\Rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\mathsf{D}(c^{T}x,c^{\prime})=m.

Proof.

The statement contains two properties to show. First, let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and let
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k}(m)
. According to the notations defined in the statement, the Euclidean division of
m
by
p
is given by the equation
m=p\cdot\xi_{p}(m)+\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(m)
. This equation gives us the following relations.

\displaystyle c^{\prime}

\displaystyle=r(m)+c^{T}x+e

\displaystyle=r(m)-p\cdot\xi_{p}(m)+c^{T}x+e+p\cdot\xi_{p}(m)

If we define the function
r^{\prime}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
with the mapping rule

m^{\prime}\mapsto r(m^{\prime}+p\cdot\xi_{p}(m))(X)-p\cdot\xi_{p}(m)

and we let
e^{\prime}
denote the polynomial
e^{\prime}(X):=e(X)+p\cdot\xi_{p}(m)
, then we have the relation

(5.5)

c^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(m))+c^{T}x+e^{\prime}

The fact that
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is a ring homomorphism gives us the following equation.

\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(r^{\prime}(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(m)))=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(r(m))-\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(p\cdot\xi_{p}(m))=m-p\cdot\xi_{p}(m)=\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(m)

For the same reasons, we have the following equation.

\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e^{\prime})=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e)+\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(p\cdot\xi_{p}(m))=p\cdot k+p\cdot\xi_{p}(m)=p\cdot(k+\xi_{p}(m))

By Definition
5.12
and Definition
5.9
, this shows that
r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{C})
and
e\in\mathcal{I}_{k+\xi_{p}(m)}(\mathsf{C})
. Equation (
5.5
) then shows that the ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})
belongs to the
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space
\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k+\xi_{p}(m)}\big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(m)\big)
.

The second property of the statement pertains to the properties of the function
\iota_{q}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}\to\mathbb{Z}
. First, let
m
be an element in the set
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and let
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k}(m)
. Recall that we have the following identity.

\mathsf{D}(c^{T}x,c^{\prime})=\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime}-c^{T}x)))

We can further expand this expression as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(c^{T}x,c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(r(m)+e)))

\displaystyle(\textrm{Definition \ref{def:encryption-space:general}})

\displaystyle=\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(r(m))+\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e)))

\displaystyle(\textrm{Proposition \ref{prop:channel-homomorphism}})

\displaystyle=\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(m+\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e)))

\displaystyle(\textrm{Definition \ref{def:arith-channel:E:errors}})

Given that
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e))\in\{0,p,2p,\dots,kp\}
for
k<(q+1)/p-1
, we have the following inequality.

\iota_{q}(m)+\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e))<p-1+((q+1)/p-1)p=q

It follows from Proposition
5.3
that the following equations hold:

\mathsf{D}(c^{T}x,c^{\prime})=\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(m))+\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e)))=m

Note that the rightmost equation comes from the equality
\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e)))=0
, since
e
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{k}(\mathsf{C})
.
∎

The following remark shows that the inequality used in the statement of Proposition
5.31
can be simplified.

Remark 5.32

(Coprimes)
.

Let
p
and
q
be two positive integers. When
p
and
q
are coprime, the condition

k<(q+1)/p-1

used in the statement of Proposition
5.31
is equivalent to the condition
k<q/p-1
. Indeed, first note that the condition
k<(q+1)/p-1
is equivalent to the condition
pk<q+1-p
, which is also equivalent to the inequality
p-1<q-pk
. Since
q
is coprime with
p
, we cannot have
q-pk=p
, hence the condition
p-1<q-pk
is equivalent to
p<q-pk
. We can then show that the latter condition is equivalent to the condition
k<q/p-1

We now define the algebraic operations for the homomorphic framework associated with ACES. Theorem
5.34
will give conditions for which these operations are stable with respect to encryption spaces.

Definition 5.33

(Algebraic operations)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
.
Also, let us consider an element
\lambda\in\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
. We define two binary operations
\oplus
and
\otimes_{\lambda}
of the form

\big(\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\big)\times\big(\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\big)\to\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}

such that the following equations hold for every
(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\in\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
and
(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\in\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\oplus(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})&=(c_{1}+c_{2},c_{1}^{\prime}+c_{2}^{\prime})\\
(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\otimes_{\lambda}(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})&=(c_{2}^{\prime}\cdot c_{1}+c_{1}^{\prime}\cdot c_{2}-c_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}c_{2},c_{1}^{\prime}c_{2}^{\prime})\end{array}

Note that the expression
c_{2}^{\prime}\cdot c_{1}+c_{1}^{\prime}\cdot c_{2}
should be seen as a linear combination of the vector
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
such that
c_{2}^{\prime}
and
c_{1}^{\prime}
are seen as scalar coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}[X]
.
The previous two equations fully determine the mapping rules associated with the operations
\oplus
and
\otimes_{\lambda}
.

As in Proposition
5.31
, Theorem
5.34
considers messages in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, enabling encryption spaces to be well-defined for the sum and product of these messages, both computed within
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. In essence, this approach leverages the definition of encryption spaces (Definition
5.29
) over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, ensuring consistency in operations like addition and multiplication.

Theorem 5.34

(Leveled FHE)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
.
Also, let us consider an element
\lambda\in\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
. For every pair
(m_{1},m_{2})
of elements in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, every pair
(k_{1},k_{2})
of non-negative integers, and every pair of ciphertexts
(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k_{1}}(m_{1}|\sigma)
and
(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k_{2}}(m_{2}|\sigma)
, the following implications hold:

\begin{array}[]{lll}k_{1}+k_{2}<q/p&\Rightarrow(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\oplus(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})&\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k_{1}+k_{2}}(m_{1}+m_{2}|\sigma)\\
(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{1}k_{2})p<q/p&\Rightarrow(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\otimes_{\lambda}(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})&\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{1}k_{2})p}(m_{1}m_{2}|\sigma)\end{array}

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward application of Definition
5.33
and Proposition
5.11
. Let us suppose that the inequality
k_{1}+k_{2}<q/p
holds and we have the following encoding for the two elements
(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k_{1}}(m_{1}|\sigma)
and
(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k_{2}}(m_{2}|\sigma)
:

(c_{1},c_{1}^{\prime})=(c_{1},r_{1}(m_{1})+c_{1}^{T}x+e_{1})\hskip 11.74988pt(c_{2},c_{2}^{\prime})=(c_{1},r_{2}(m_{2})+c_{2}^{T}x+e_{2})

Let us denote
e_{3}=e_{1}+e_{2}
. Since
k_{1}+k_{2}<q/p
, with
e_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}}(\mathsf{C})
and
e_{2}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}}(\mathsf{C})
, Proposition
5.11
implies that
e_{3}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}(\mathsf{C})
. We can use the previous formulas to show that there exists
r\in\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{C})
for which the following equations hold:

\displaystyle(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\oplus(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle=(c_{1}+c_{2},r_{1}(m_{1})+r_{2}(m_{2})+(c_{1}+c_{2})^{T}x+e_{1}+e_{2})

\displaystyle(\textrm{linearity})

\displaystyle=(c_{1}+c_{2},r(m_{1}+m_{2})+(c_{1}+c_{2})^{T}x+e_{3})

\displaystyle(\textrm{Proposition \ref{prop:homomorphic-properties:E:errors}})

According to Definition
5.29
, the previous equations show that
(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\oplus(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})
is in
\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k_{1}+k_{2}}(m_{1}+m_{2}|\sigma)
.

To show the property satisfied by the operation
\otimes_{\lambda}
, we will proceed in two steps. First, suppose that
k_{1}k_{2}p<q/p
and consider the element
e_{3}=e_{2}r_{1}(m_{1})+e_{1}r_{2}(m_{2})+e_{1}e_{2}
. Since we have
e_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}}(\mathsf{C})
and
e_{2}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}}(\mathsf{C})
, and the images of the polynomials
r(m_{1})
and
r(m_{2})
via the function
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}
land in
\{0,1,\dots,p\}
, Proposition
5.11
implies that we have the relations
e_{2}r_{1}(m_{1})\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}p}(\mathsf{C})
,
e_{1}r_{2}(m_{2})\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}p}(\mathsf{C})
and
e_{1}e_{2}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}k_{2}p}(\mathsf{C})
. It follows from the first item of Proposition
5.11
that the relation
e_{3}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}p+k_{2}p+k_{1}k_{2}p}(\mathsf{C})
holds. Then, we can use Proposition
5.14
and the expression of the element
e_{3}
to show that there exists
r\in\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{C})
for which the following equations hold:

\displaystyle(r_{1}(m_{1})+e_{1})(r_{2}(m_{2})+e_{2})

\displaystyle=r_{1}(m_{1})r_{2}(m_{2})+e_{3}

\displaystyle=r(m_{1}m_{2})+e_{3}

\displaystyle(\textrm{Proposition \ref{prop:homomorphic-properties:E:errors}})

Alternatively, Proposition
5.28
gives us an element
e_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
for which the following identities hold.

\displaystyle(r_{1}(m_{1})+e_{1})(r_{2}(m_{2})+e_{2})

\displaystyle=(c^{\prime}_{1}-c_{1}^{T}x)(c^{\prime}_{2}-c_{2}^{T}x)

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}-\big(c^{\prime}_{2}\cdot c_{1}+c^{\prime}_{1}\cdot c_{2}\big)^{T}x+(c_{1}^{T}x)(c_{2}^{T}x)

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}-\big(c^{\prime}_{2}\cdot c_{1}+c^{\prime}_{1}\cdot c_{2}\big)^{T}x+\big(c_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}c_{2}\big)^{T}x+e_{4}

\displaystyle(\textrm{Proposition \ref{prop:boxtimes_lambda:product}})

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}-\big(c^{\prime}_{2}\cdot c_{1}+c^{\prime}_{1}\cdot c_{2}-c_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}c_{2}\big)^{T}x+e_{4}

\displaystyle(\textrm{linearity})

Our two previous developments give us the following equation:

(5.6)

c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}=r(m_{1}m_{2})+\big(c^{\prime}_{2}\cdot c_{1}+c^{\prime}_{1}\cdot c_{2}-c_{1}\boxtimes_{\lambda}c_{2}\big)^{T}x+e_{3}+e_{4}

According to Proposition
5.28
, the element
e_{4}
is such that
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e_{4})=0
, which means that we have the identity

\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e_{4}))=\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(e_{3}+e_{4}))

and hence the following relation:

e_{3}+e_{4}\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}p+k_{2}p+k_{1}k_{2}p}(\mathsf{C})

It follows from Definition
5.29
that equation (
5.6
) is equivalent to the statement that
(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\otimes_{\lambda}(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})
is in
\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{1}k_{2})p}(m_{1}m_{2}|\sigma)
.
∎

In summary of this section, the assertions made in Theorem
5.34
and Proposition
5.31
affirm that the encryption scheme introduced in section
5.1
qualifies as a leveled fully homomorphic encryption scheme. This holds true under the condition that
\mathsf{Alice}
publishes an element of the set
\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
, where
x
represents the private key of the encryption scheme.

5.4.
Refreshable ciphertexts

This section introduces the concept of
refreshable ciphertexts
, which will be instrumental in section
5.5
to demonstrate that ACES enables a fully homomorphic encryption scheme.
Throughout this section and section
5.5
, we adopt the following convention to streamline notation:

Convention 5.35

(Notation)
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. For every function
f:A\to B
and every
n
-tuple
a=(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})
of elements in
A
, denote by
f(\!(a)\!)
the corresponding
n
-tuple
(f(a_{1}),f(a_{2}),\dots,f(a_{n}))
of elements in
B
.

The following convention assigns two levels of meaning to the term
pseudociphertext
. The first level refers to a generic terminology, primarily used in relation to an ACES secret key (see Definition
5.37
). The second specifically refers to a pseudociphertext associated with an ACES ciphertext (defined in Definition
5.29
). Most of our results will only require the first level of meaning until we reach Theorem
5.46
.

Convention 5.36

(Pseudociphertexts)
.

For any positive integer
q
, we refer to a pair
(v,v^{\prime})
, where
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}
and
v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, as a
q
-pseudociphertext
. Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel,
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For any element
m
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and any non-negative integer
k
, we define the
underlying
q
-pseudociphertext
of a given ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k}^{x}(m|\sigma)
as the following
q
-pseudociphertext:

\Big(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(-c)\!),\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime})\Big).

Here, the
n
-tuple
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(-c)\!)
is the tuple whose
i
-th coefficient is given by the following element in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(-c_{i})=-\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c_{i})=\pi_{q}(q-\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c_{i})))

The following definition is justified by a lack of homomorphism in the function
\iota_{q}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, as suggested in Proposition
5.3
and illustrated in Example
5.4
. Specifically, the function
\iota_{q}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is only homomorphic up to a multiple of
q
and the following definition targets cases in which this homomorphic property holds up to some multiple of
p\cdot q
for some positive integer
p
.

Definition 5.37

(Refreshable pseudociphertexts)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
n
be a positive integer and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every non-negative integer
k
, we define the
k
-th space of
p
-refreshable
q
-pseudociphertexts
relative to
x
as:

\mathcal{F}_{p,q}^{k}(x)=\{(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}~|~\iota_{q}(v^{\prime})+\iota_{q}(\!(v)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)=\iota_{q}\big(v^{\prime}+v^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)+kpq\}

Every
q
-pseudociphertext in the set
\mathcal{F}_{p,q}^{k}(x)
is said to be
p
-refreshable
.

Proposition
5.38
(stated below) gives a context in which the function
\iota_{q}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
can be seen as a ring homomorphism (up to composition) relative to the algebraic operations used in the decryption algorithm of ACES (this will be furthered discussed in Proposition
5.40
).

Proposition 5.38

(Refreshable pseudociphertexts)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
n
be a positive integer and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every non-negative integer
k
and every element
(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}_{p,q}^{k}(x)
, the following equation holds in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(v^{\prime})+\Big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(\!(v)\!)\Big)^{T}\Big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\Big)=\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\big(v^{\prime}+v^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)

Proof.

Since we have
(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}_{p,q}^{k}(x)
, the following relation holds.

\iota_{q}(v^{\prime})+\iota_{q}(\!(v)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)=\iota_{q}(v^{\prime}+v^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!))+kpq

By applying the ring homomorphism
\pi_{p}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
on the previous equation, we obtain the following relation.

\pi_{p}(\iota_{q}(v^{\prime}))+\pi_{p}(\!(\iota_{q}(\!(v)\!))\!)^{T}\pi_{p}(\!(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!))\!)=\pi_{p}\big(\iota_{q}\big(v^{\prime}+v^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\big)+0

It then follows from composing the function
\pi_{p}
with
\iota_{q}
that the equation of the statement holds.
∎

Definition 5.39

(Refreshable ciphertexts)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel,
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every element
m
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and every non-negative integer
k
, we will say that a ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k}^{x}(m|\sigma)
is
p
-refreshable
if so is its underlying
q
-pseudociphertext.

The following proposition constitutes the main application of Definition
5.39
. Specifically, it suggests that refreshable ciphertexts can be decrypted with reduced knowledge of the secret key. Importantly, this information is unlikely to be inferred from the published data of ACES due to the absence of homomorphic properties in
\iota_{q}
. In fact, this feature is one of the main components ensuring the security of ACES.

Proposition 5.40

(Refreshable ciphertexts)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel,
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every element
m
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, every non-negative integer
k<(q+1)/p-1
, and every
p
-refreshable ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k}^{x}(m|\sigma)
, the following equation holds in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime}))+\Big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(-c)\!)\Big)^{T}\Big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\Big)=m

Proof.

By Definition
5.39
, the
q
-pseudociphertext
(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(-c)\!),\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime}))
is
p
-refreshable. It follows from Proposition
5.38
that the top equation displayed below holds. The other equations underneath it are deduced directly from the homomorphic properties of
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}
and the definition of the decryption algorithm for ACES (see Generation
5.15
).

\displaystyle\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime}))+\Big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(-c)\!)\Big)^{T}\Big(\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\Big)

\displaystyle=\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\Big(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime})+\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\Big)

\displaystyle=\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}(\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime}-c^{T}x))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}(c^{T}x,c^{\prime})

Since
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and we assumed that the inequality
k<(q+1)/p-1
holds, Proposition
5.31
implies that the equation
\mathsf{D}(c^{T}x,c^{\prime})=m
holds. This shows the statement.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we demonstrate how it is possible to determine whether a given ciphertext is refreshable without knowledge of the secret key. To achieve this, we introduce
locators
(Definition
5.41
) and
directors
(Definition
5.42
), which define an affine space in which ciphertext components can be expressed. This decomposition may resemble Gentry’s squashing technique
[
14
]
, which also aims to refresh ciphertexts through decomposition. However, a key distinction is that our method targets the ciphertext itself, whereas squashing decomposes secret-key information, exposing it to greater security risks.

Definition 5.41

(Locators)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
n
be a positive integer and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every non-negative integer
k
, we define the
k
-th space of
p
-locators
relative to
x
as:

\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{k}(x)=\left\{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}~\left|~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})-\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor=kp\right.\right\}

Every
n
-tuple in the set
\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{k}(x)
is said to be a
p
-locator for
x
.

While locators describe structured sets of elements that characterize possible values of a ciphertext component, directors represent shifts within this space. A locator combined with directors can form another locator, allowing structured decomposition.

Definition 5.42

(Directors)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
n
be a positive integer and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every non-negative integer
k
, we define the
k
-th space of
p
-directors
relative to
x
as:

\mathcal{D}_{p,q}^{k}(x)=\left\{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}~\left|~\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor=kp\right.\right\}

Every
n
-tuple in the set
\mathcal{D}_{p,q}^{k}(x)
is said to be a
p
-director for
x
.

The following definition introduces the concept of a
margin
, which quantifies how much flexibility there is in combining locators and directors within the affine structure.

Definition 5.43

(Margin)
.

Let
n
and
q
be a positive integers and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every element
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}
, we define the
margin
of
\ell
relative to
x
as the real number:

\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell)=\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)-\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor

Note that
\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell)
belongs to the interval
[0,1)
of non-negative real number less than
1
.

The following proposition gives conditions in which it is possible to combine a locator with a collection of directors to obtain a new locator.

Proposition 5.44

(Affine structure)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
n
be a positive integer, and let
x
be an element of
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every non-negative integer
r
, let
\square_{i}
denote either addition or subtraction, and let
\widetilde{\square}_{i}
denote its corresponding inverse operation. Define the following expressions:

F(x_{0},\dots,x_{r})=x_{0}{\,\square_{1}\,}x_{1}{\,\square_{2}\,}\dots{\,\square_{r}\,}x_{r},\hskip 11.74988pt\textrm{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\widetilde{F}(x_{0},\dots,x_{r})=x_{0}{\,\widetilde{\square}_{1}\,}x_{1}{\,\widetilde{\square}_{2}\,}\dots{\,\widetilde{\square}_{r}\,}x_{r}.

For every pair
(k_{0},k_{1})
of non-negative integers, every
p
-locator
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{k_{0}}(x)
, and every collection of
p
-directors
\delta_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{p,q}^{k_{1}}(x),\dots,\delta_{r}\in\mathcal{D}_{p,q}^{k_{r}}(x)
, suppose that the expression
F(\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!),\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{1})\!),\dots,\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{r})\!))
, computed in
\mathbb{Z}^{(n)}
, belongs to
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}
and that there exists a non-negative integer
k^{\prime}
such that

F(\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell),\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{r}))\in[pk^{\prime},pk^{\prime}+1)

Then, the following hold:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}F(\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell),\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{r}))-pk^{\prime}=\mathsf{marg}_{x}(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})),\\
F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})\in\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{\widetilde{F}(k_{0},k_{1},\dots,k_{r})-k^{\prime}}(x).\end{array}\right.

Proof.

We assume that the conditions of the proposition are satisfied. By Proposition
5.3
, if the expression
F(\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!),\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{1})\!),\dots,\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{r})\!))
belongs to
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}
, then we have the identity:

(5.7)

F(\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!),\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{1})\!),\dots,\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{r})\!))=\iota_{q}(\!(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}))\!).

Note that in equation (
5.7
), the term
F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})
is implicitly computed in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n)}
, since the domain of
\iota_{q}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. We now show that the following relation holds:

F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})\in\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{\widetilde{F}(k_{0},k_{1},\dots,k_{r})-k^{\prime}}(x).

From equation (
5.7
) and Definition
5.43
, we obtain:

\displaystyle F(\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell),\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{r}))=\frac{1}{q}\cdot\big(\iota_{q}(\!(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}))\!)\big)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)-\dots

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988ptF\left(\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor,\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{1})\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor,\dots,\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{r})\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor\right).

Using the definition of the margin of
F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})
along with the previous equation, we derive the identity:

\displaystyle F(\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell),\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{r}))-\mathsf{marg}_{x}(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}))=\dots

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988ptF\left(\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor,\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{1})\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor,\dots,\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{r})\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor\right)-\dots

\displaystyle\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\hskip 11.74988pt\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}))\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor.

On the left-hand side, we have a real number in the open interval
(pk^{\prime}-1,pk^{\prime}+1)
, while on the right-hand side, we have an integer. Since the only integer in this interval is
pk^{\prime}
, we deduce that:

F(\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell),\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\delta_{r}))=\mathsf{marg}_{x}(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}))+pk^{\prime}.

Thus, the following decomposition holds:

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}))\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor=\dots

\displaystyle F\left(\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor,\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{1})\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor,\dots,\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(\delta_{r})\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor\right)-pk^{\prime}.

Since
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{k_{0}}(x)
and
\delta_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{p,q}^{k_{1}}(x),\dots,\delta_{r}\in\mathcal{D}_{p,q}^{k_{r}}(x)
, it follows from the previous equation and Definitions
5.41
and
5.42
that:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})-\left\lfloor\frac{1}{q}\cdot\iota_{q}(\!(F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r}))\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)\right\rfloor=(\widetilde{F}(k_{0},k_{1},\dots,k_{r})-k^{\prime})p.

Thus, we conclude that
F(\ell,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{r})\in\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{\widetilde{F}(k_{0},k_{1},\dots,k_{r})-k^{\prime}}(x)
, which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.45

(Affine structure)
.

In Theorem
5.46
(see below), we will see that a ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})
, relative to some arithmetic channel
\mathsf{C}
, is refreshable if the vector
\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(c)\!)
is a locator. This characterization motivates the need for a test to determine whether a given
c
is a locator. Our proposed approach is to attempt to decompose
c
as

c=\ell+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\pm\delta_{i},

where
\ell
is a locator and the terms
\delta_{i}
are directors. If such a decomposition is found, then by Proposition
5.44
, we can conclude that
c
is indeed a locator, provided that the conditions of the proposition are satisfied.

One way to find this decomposition is by solving a matrix equation
Ad=c
in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, where the matrix
A
encodes a database of preselected locators and directors. However, it is important to note that this test is not exhaustive: failure to find such a decomposition does not necessarily mean that
c
is not a locator. The effectiveness of the test depends on the choice of stored vectors, which should ideally form a basis sufficient to represent the space of locators.

We conclude this section with Theorem
5.46
, which provides a straightforward test for verifying whether ACES ciphertexts are refreshable.

Theorem 5.46
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
n
be a positive integer, and let
x
be an element of
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every element
m
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, every non-negative integer
k
, and every ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k}^{x}(m|\sigma)
, if there exists a non-negative integer
k_{0}
such that
\ell=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(c)\!)\in\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{k_{0}}(x)
, then the following implication holds:

\frac{p\cdot(k+1)-1}{q}<1-\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell)\hskip 11.74988pt\Rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt(c,c^{\prime})\textrm{ is $p$-refreshable}.

Proof.

We assume that the conditions
\ell=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(c)\!)\in\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{k_{0}}(x)
and
\frac{p(k+1)-1}{q}<1-\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell)
hold. Let us denote
z=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime})
. To prove the theorem, we need to show that the following equation holds:

(5.8)

\iota_{q}(z)+\iota_{q}(\!(-\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)=\iota_{q}\big(z-\ell^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)+k_{0}pq.

Before proving this equation, observe that our assumption
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k}^{x}(m|\sigma)
, where
z=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime})
and
\ell=\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(c)\!)
, gives the identity:

(5.9)

z=\pi_{q}\Big(m+\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)+kp\Big).

We will use expression (
5.9
) to establish both sides of equation (
5.8
). We begin by reformulating the right-hand side.

First, note that the inequality
\frac{p(k+1)-1}{q}<1-\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell)
simplifies to
pk+(p-1)<q
. This implies that for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, the sum
m+pk
remains in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. By Definition
5.1
, we obtain the following equations:

\displaystyle\iota_{q}\big(z-\ell^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)

\displaystyle=\iota_{q}\Big(m+\ell^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)+kp-\ell^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\Big)

\displaystyle(\text{from equation (\ref{eq:refreshable:euclidean:v_prime:z_expression})})

\displaystyle=\iota_{q}(m+kp)

\displaystyle=m+k_{1}p

\displaystyle(\text{since }m+kp<q).

This provides the desired reformulation for the right-hand side of equation (
5.8
).

Next, we reformulate the left-hand side of equation (
5.8
). Note that equation (
5.9
) expresses
z
as an image of the quotient map
\pi_{q}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Specifically, this implies that
z
is the remainder in the Euclidean division of
m+\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)+kp
by
q
. Therefore, we have:

(5.10)

m+\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)+kp=q\left\lfloor\frac{\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)+m+kp}{q}\right\rfloor+\iota_{q}(z).

Since we have the inequality
\frac{kp+m}{q}<1-\mathsf{marg}_{x}(\ell)
, it follows from Definition
5.43
that we can simplify the floored term in equation (
5.10
) as:

(5.11)

\left\lfloor\frac{\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)+m+kp}{q}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\frac{\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)}{q}\right\rfloor.

Also, observe that the identity
\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}+\iota_{q}(\!(-\ell)\!)^{T}=(q,q,\dots,q)
holds. Using equation (
5.11
), we simplify equation (
5.10
) as follows:

m+q\sum_{i}\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})+kp=q\left\lfloor\frac{\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)}{q}\right\rfloor+\iota_{q}(z)+\iota_{q}(\!(-\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big).

This simplifies further to:

(5.12)

\iota_{q}(z)+\iota_{q}(\!(-\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)=m+kp+q\left(\sum_{i}\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})-\left\lfloor\frac{\iota_{q}(\!(\ell)\!)^{T}\big(\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)}{q}\right\rfloor\right).

Since
\ell
is a
p
-locator in
\mathcal{L}_{p,q}^{k_{0}}(x)
(by Definition
5.41
), the term inside the large parentheses in equation (
5.12
) is equal to
k_{0}p
. Furthermore, since we showed that
\iota_{q}\big(z-\ell^{T}\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}C\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(\!(x)\!)\big)=m+kp
, equation (
5.12
) is equivalent to equation (
5.8
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.47

(Non-refreshable ciphertexts)
.

So far, we have not discussed cases where a ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})
is not refreshable. If such a situation occurs, we may want to transform it into a refreshable ciphertext while preserving the encoded message. One way to achieve this is by applying neutral homomorphic operations to
(c,c^{\prime})
so that the resulting ciphertext still encrypts the same message. For example, let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x
be an element of
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. For every element
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and every non-
p
-refreshable ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k}^{x}(m|\sigma)
, one can attempt to find another ciphertext
(c_{0},c^{\prime}_{0})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k_{0}}^{x}(0|\sigma)
such that the sum
(c_{0},c^{\prime}_{0})\oplus(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k_{0}+k}^{x}(m|\sigma)
is
p
-refreshable.

5.5.
From leveled FHE schemes to proper FHE schemes

This section introduces a refresh operation designed to decrease the encryption level linked to a ciphertext. First, let us extend our set of algebraic operations defined in Definition
5.33
with a scalar product.

The following definition uses the implicit fact that the addition operation
\oplus
defined in Definition
5.33
is associative and commutative.

Definition 5.48

(Scalar product)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. Also, let us consider an element
\lambda\in\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
. For every positive integer
n_{0}
, consider the following:

1)

an
n_{0}
-tuple
\gamma_{1}=((c_{1,i},c_{1,i}^{\prime}))_{i\in[n_{0}]}
of elements in
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
;

2)

an
n_{0}
-tuple
\gamma_{2}=((c_{2,i},c_{2,i}^{\prime}))_{i\in[n_{0}]}
of elements in
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
.

We define the
\lambda
-product of
\gamma_{1}
with
\gamma_{2}
as the following element
\gamma_{1}\odot_{\lambda}\gamma_{2}
defined in
\sigma\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}\odot_{\lambda}\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=\big((c_{1,1},c_{1,1}^{\prime})\otimes_{\lambda}(c_{2,1},c_{2,1}^{\prime})\big)\oplus\dots\oplus\big((c_{1,n_{0}},c_{1,n_{0}}^{\prime})\otimes_{\lambda}(c_{2,n_{0}},c_{2,n_{0}}^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n_{0}}(c_{1,i},c_{1,i}^{\prime})\otimes_{\lambda}(c_{2,i},c_{2,i}^{\prime})

The following definition introduces a sequence of ciphertexts, called
refresher
, that encrypts transformed information related to the secret key. This transformation reduces the risk of revealing the secret key and eliminates the need for deep circuit evaluation by matching the simplicity of the base decryption algorithm. The refresher structure also diverges from conventional bootstrapping techniques, as it is expected to use the general ciphertext formula from Definition
5.29
, rather than the public-key-dependent encryption used in traditional bootstrapping. For these reasons, our approach notably differs from conventional bootstrapping techniques.

Definition 5.49

(Refresher)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. We define a
refresher
for the pair
(\mathsf{C},x)
as an
n
-sequence
(\rho_{1},\rho_{1}^{\prime}),(\rho_{2},\rho_{2}^{\prime}),\dots,(\rho_{n},\rho_{n}^{\prime})
of ciphertexts and an
n
-sequence
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\dots,\kappa_{n}
of non-negative integers such that for every
i\in[n]
, the following relation holds:

(\rho_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},\kappa_{i}}^{x}\big(\pi_{q}\circ\iota_{p}\circ\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(x_{i})\big|\sigma\big)

Such a refresher structure will be denoted as a pair
(\kappa,\varrho)
where
\kappa
denotes the
n
-vector
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\dots,\kappa_{n})
and
\varrho
denotes the
n
-vector
\big((\rho_{1},\rho_{1}^{\prime}),(\rho_{2},\rho_{2}^{\prime}),\dots,(\rho_{n},\rho_{n}^{\prime})\big)
.

The main idea behind Theorem
5.50
(stated below) is to extend the result of Proposition
5.40
to the type of information made available by a refresher (Definition
5.49
).

Theorem 5.50

(Proper FHE)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(p,q,\omega,u)
be an arithmetic channel, let
\sigma
be an
n
-repartition of
q
, and let
x
be an element in
\mathbb{Z}[X]^{(n)}
. Also, let us consider
\lambda\in\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
and a refresher
(\kappa,\varrho)
for
(\mathsf{C},x)
. For every
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, every non-negative integer
k<(q+1)/p-1
, and every
p
-refreshable ciphertext
(c,c^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{x}_{\mathsf{C},k}(m|\sigma)
, let
\gamma=((c_{1,i},c_{1,i}^{\prime}))_{i\in[n]}
where

(c_{1,i},c_{1,i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k_{1,i}}^{x}\big(\pi_{q}\circ\iota_{p}\circ\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(-c_{i})\big|\sigma\big)\textrm{ for some integer }k_{1,i},

and take

(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},k_{2}}^{x}\big(\pi_{q}\circ\iota_{p}\circ\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}(c^{\prime})\big|\sigma\big)\textrm{ for some integer }k_{2}.

If we let

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\kappa_{\ast}&=\displaystyle k_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}p\cdot(\kappa_{i}+k_{1,i}+\kappa_{i}\cdot k_{1,i})\\
\kappa^{\ast}&=\displaystyle\left\lfloor\frac{(p-1)+n(p-1)^{2}}{p}\right\rfloor\end{array}\right.

and assume that
\kappa_{\ast}<q/p
, then the ciphertext
(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\oplus(\gamma\odot_{\lambda}\varrho)
belongs to the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},\kappa_{\ast}+\kappa^{\ast}}^{x}(m|\sigma)
.

Proof.

For convenience, we will use the following notations:

\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma_{1}=\pi_{q}\circ\iota_{p}\circ\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
\Gamma_{2}=\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\circ\textrm{\textlbrackdbl}\mathsf{C}\textrm{\textrbrackdbl}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}\\
\end{array}

Now, since, for every integer
i\in[n]
, the inequality

p\cdot(k_{1,i}+\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i}\cdot k_{1,i})\leq\kappa_{\ast}<q/p

holds, Theorem
5.34
implies that the ciphertext
(c_{1,i},c_{1,i}^{\prime})\otimes_{\lambda}(\rho_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime})
is in the
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space

\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},p\cdot(k_{1,i}+\kappa_{i}+\kappa_{i}k_{1,i})}^{x}\Big(\Gamma_{1}(-c_{i})\cdot\Gamma_{1}(x_{i})|\sigma\Big).

Similarly, since the inequality

\sum_{i=1}^{n}p\cdot(\kappa_{i}+k_{1,i}+\kappa_{i}k_{1,i})\leq\kappa_{\ast}<q/p

holds, Theorem
5.34
and Definition
5.48
implies that the ciphertext
\gamma\odot_{\lambda}\rho
is in the
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space

\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p\cdot(\kappa_{i}+k_{1,i}+\kappa_{i}k_{1,i}))}^{x}\Big(\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!)|\sigma\Big).

Again, since the inequality

k_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}p\cdot(\kappa_{i}+k_{1,i}+\kappa_{i}k_{1,i})=\kappa_{\ast}<q/p

holds, Theorem
5.34
implies that the ciphertext
(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\oplus\big(\gamma\odot_{\lambda}\varrho\big)
belongs to the
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space

\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},\kappa_{\ast}}^{x}\Big(\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!)|\sigma\Big).

It follows from the homomorphic properties of the ring homomorphism
\pi_{q}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
that the following equation holds.

\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\cdot\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!)=\pi_{q}\Big(\Gamma_{2}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{2}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\cdot\Gamma_{2}(\!(x)\!)\Big)

As a result, applying the composite function
\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
on the previous equation gives the following identity.

\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\Big(\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\cdot\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!)\Big)=\Gamma_{2}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{2}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\cdot\Gamma_{2}(\!(x)\!)

Since
(c,c^{\prime})
is
p
-refreshable, and we took
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
k<(q+1)/p-1
, it follows from Proposition
5.40
that the following identity holds.

\pi_{p}\circ\iota_{q}\Big(\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\cdot\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!)\Big)=m

It follows from the first part of the statement of Proposition
5.31
that the ciphertext
(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\oplus\big(\gamma\odot_{\lambda}\varrho\big)
belongs to the
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space

\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},\kappa_{\ast}+\xi_{p}(\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!))}^{x}\Big(m|\sigma\Big).

Now, recall that the function
\Gamma_{1}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}[X]_{u}\to\mathbb{Z}
factors through the function
\pi_{q}\circ\iota_{p}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. As a result, the images of
\Gamma_{1}
are less than or equal to
p-1
. We deduce from this that the following inequality holds.

\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!)\leq(p-1)+n(p-1)^{2}

This means that the integer quotient
\xi_{p}(\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!))
of the element
\Gamma_{1}(c^{\prime})+\Gamma_{1}(\!(-c)\!)^{T}\Gamma_{1}(\!(x)\!)
by
p
is bounded from above by the quantity
\kappa^{\ast}
. In other words, the ciphertext
(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\oplus\big(\gamma\odot_{\lambda}\varrho\big)
belongs to the
\mathsf{C}
-encryption space
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{C},\kappa_{\ast}+\kappa^{\ast}}^{x}(m|\sigma)
.
∎

To conclude, the combined statements of Theorem
5.34
, Proposition
5.31
and Theorem
5.50
show that the encryption scheme defined in section
5.1
defines a proper fully homomorphic encryption schemes.

5.6.
Security

The security of the cryptosystem outlined in section
5.1
is contingent upon the computational complexity of the LWE problem (see
[
26
]
). Specifically, the challenge lies in the difficulty of deducing the secret key
x
, as defined in Generation
5.15
, solely from the knowledge of the public key
(f,f^{\prime})
provided in Publication
5.17
. Breaking an ACES ciphertext would also effectively require to solve the LWE problem in integers (through the homomorphism of Proposition
5.6
). Here, we make the assumption that the publication of a
3
-tensor
\lambda
from the set
\sigma\mathcal{H}(x|\mathsf{C},\sigma)
(defined in Definition
5.26
) does not provide enough information about the components of
x
to compromise the secure full homomorphic property of ACES.

5.7.
Implementation

The cryptosystem described in section
5.1
has an existing Python implementation tailored for the parameter value
\omega=1
. While this implementation features a user-friendly suite of functions, it is important to note that it is not optimized for real-world applications. Its design is geared towards facilitating research and experimentation. The code for this package can be accessed on GitHub through the following link:
https://github.com/remytuyeras/aces
.
